#5007. Jessica McClain's Approach to Running Breaks a Lot of the Usual Rules. It's Working.

The Brooks athlete spoke to Women’s Health fresh off the NYC Marathon.

After a major marathon, you hear about two different podiums. There’s the actual podium, made up of the top three finishers, and then another, unofficial one of the fastest Americans. That’s because, though there’s some incredible talent in the U.S., oftentimes, it’s simply not enough to beat out international runners, including on our own courses. In the New York City Marathon women’s race, for example, an American hasn’t been in the top three for eight years. (In the men’s race, it’s been since 2009.)

That streak ended this Sunday when Brooks athlete Jessica McClain crossed the finish line in 2:24:42, coming in at second place. Even more exciting: She was poised to get third but overtook last year’s winner Hellen Obiri in the final meters. She did it looking effortless, with a pair of Goodr sunglasses in her hand and a huge smile on her face.

Even before the race cannon went off that morning, her momentum had been building. In the spring, McClain placed fifth in the Boston Marathon at 2:20:49 and set an American course record. Going into New York, she initially wanted to be in the top five again. “But then I was like, ‘Wait, I want to do better than I did in Boston, and that's fourth, and I do not want fourth place,’” she tells Women’s Health. So, it was settled: She was shooting for third or higher.

After a performance like she had in New York, you can expect that’s always going to be the goal now for the 34-year-old. “I think ‘top American’ just isn't going to cut it anymore for where I want to be on the international stage,” she says. “I want to compete and I want to practice competing, especially because we're coming up on the Olympic trials, and the top three go to the Olympics. I want to practice competing for those top three spots no matter the race.”

Heading into NYC, McClain was feeling relaxed. The race started out hard, she remembers, so she just focused on getting her fuel in at each aid station and taking the run mile by mile. “I was just checking boxes and staying out of trouble as much as I could the first 13 to 16 miles,” she says. Then, once she got to Manhattan, the pack started to run faster, her gels started to kick in, and she started to feel better. Still, it wasn’t until the last 10K when the running started to feel really good.

McClain was strategic until the end—running the tangents and taking the shortest turns possible to close the gap between Obiri and first-place finisher Sheila Chepkirui, who she noticed were making their turns a little wider and slower. Even down to the last stretch turning into Central Park, second wasn’t exactly on McClain’s mind. She was just thrilled to take third. She took off her sunglasses to take it all in, which is why they were in her hand as she crossed the finish line.

“Then I saw Hellen looking around and was like, ‘Oh my God, wait, I think I can get second,’” she says. So, she dug deep, pumped her arms, and fed off the crowd’s energy. “I just felt really, really good,” she says. “I've run a lot of reps at the end of a lot of hard workouts that were fast and I was like, ‘I can do this.’” The result was one of the most exciting second-place finishes to a marathon in a long time.

Despite her headline-grabbing performances in 2026, it was just a few years ago that McClain stepped away from professional running after hitting what she describes as “rock bottom.” She was chronically injured, having dealt with her share of tight tendons, stress fractures, plantar issues, and strained muscles, and unable to race at her full potential. So, she moved home to Arizona, got a full-time job in the nonprofit sector, and reevaluated her relationship with running.

“It gave me a lot of perspective,” McClain says. “For a long time I was very outcome oriented, not process driven. It was a lot about ‘We need to hit these times and get this place,’ and I wasn't enjoying the ride to get there, and it trickled into a lot of other things in life.”

From 2020 to 2022, McClain just ran for herself. She didn’t have a super formal plan or even a coach. She worked full time, met the man who is now her husband, reconnected with old friends, and had fun outside of her sport. “I needed that, but naturally found my way back [to running],” she says. She signed up for the Mesa Marathon in February 2022 to celebrate her 30th birthday—and won it.

Slowly, she started racing again, even self-coaching her way to a fourth-place performance in the 2024 Olympic marathon trials. Now, she’s resigned with Brooks and back to racing as a professional, but she still maintains all those other parts of herself she gained in her time off. That period away from professional running didn’t sabotage her progress. Instead, it set her up for this latest era, one where she’s happier—and more successful—than ever.

Stepping away from professional running led to a mindset shift for McClain. First, she prioritizes fun—and you can tell. Obviously, coming off of an incredible finish at one of the world’s biggest marathons will put you in a good mood, but it’s clear that McClain is enthusiastic about running. She leads with such a pure love for the sport that pros (and amateurs) can lose sight of, that she had once lost sight of until her time away.

“This is supposed to be fun,” McClain says. “Running is amazing, but at the end of the day, it's just running and there's so much else in life. I just want to enjoy it and to get the best out of myself and make the sport a little bit better, whatever that means.”

That philosophy carries over into her day-to-day training, where she prioritizes workouts she actually enjoys. “Eighty-five percent of the work I do gives me confidence, and then there's 15 percent that's really hard that I don't love, but that's good for me,” McClain says. She’s also not a cross-trainer. “It does not bring me joy,” McClain says. “I hate swimming. I hate biking.” Yes, even if she gets injured. When it comes to strength training, she keeps that minimal too.

Similarly, you’ll rarely catch McClain wrapping up a session doubled over and exhausted, she says. “I’m never hitting a home run in practice,” she says. “That’s for racing.”

Due to her full-time job as the executive director at the Johnjay & Rich #LoveUp Foundation, her weekly mileage also leans a little shorter than some of her competitors. (During this training block, McClain ran about 72 to 84 miles every week, with one hard session and one long run per week in addition to her easy miles.) Her coach, David Roche, also doesn’t assign runs based on paces. Instead, they focus on effort.

Beyond keeping training from getting too draining, McClain has seen another incredible benefit from this approach: She’s been injury-free for nearly four years. “That's something that just gives me a little bit of an edge,” she says. “I've had a lot of healthy blocks strung together.”

When it comes to nutrition, McClain takes the same refreshingly uncomplicated approach, and it’s paying off. “I just feel like I’m the strongest athlete I’ve ever been,” she says. It’s all very intuitive when it comes to deciding what to eat. You won’t catch her planning out meals or counting macros. There’s nothing super strict or specific to her diet. She eats what she wants and relies on snacks to keep her well-fed. “I’m never hungry,” she says.

Plus, her fueling plan doesn’t come in between her and living life. “In the off season and when my season isn't super crazy, I go out with friends, I have fun, I go to happy hour. I don't try to live in restriction or super high monitoring ever,” McClain says. When she gets closer to race day, she dials in a bit more, but since she’s been so relaxed otherwise, it just makes it fun to lock in for a month.

There is one food that’s become a mainstay in her diet: Burgers—two to three nights a week. “It's super easy,” McClain says. “Throw it on the grill, slap it between two buns and put whatever you want on it. It's great and I get a ton of protein.” Her sweet treat of choice is Dairy Queen, a spot she frequents.

Next, McClain has her sights set on the Olympics. She placed fourth at the last trials, making her an alternate for Paris but just missing the chance to race for the country.

In the meantime, as she waits for 2028, she’s going to keep crushing the marathon and competing for every podium. “It's taken a long time to get to the point where I look at these women—and they're women I've been watching and idolizing in the marathon for quite some time—and I'm like, ‘Oh no, I can race with them too,’” she says. But now, there’s no doubting that she can.

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

#5005. Bitcoin’s ‘best buying opportunity ever’ has arrived: Mark Moss

Bitcoin Opportunity Fund co-founder Mark Moss says that with banks and ETFs widening access to Bitcoin he expects a new record high in 2027.

Wall Street’s embrace of Bitcoin has created the “best buying opportunity ever,” according to investor Mark Moss.

Moss co-founded the Bitcoin Opportunity Fund, where he is an advisory partner. In 2025, UK-listed Satsuma Technology appointed him chief Bitcoin strategist.

Moss tells Cointelegraph on the latest episode of Trade Secrets that he first bought Bitcoin in 2015 at about $300, during a recovery from a $1,000 to $200 plunge.

“It was so risky,” Moss recalls of buying back then. In contrast, today, “you have consensus from the US government and Wall Street.”

“But most people still think it’s a scam,” he says.

That skepticism opens buying opportunities as Bitcoin starts to cross what Moss calls the “chasm” between early adopters and mainstream investors. Banks and exchange-traded funds (ETFs) have made Bitcoin easier to buy and financial advisers could help persuade clients who have stayed away previously to consider investing.

Related: Strategy shareholders approve daily dividends for four preferred stocks

Bitcoin briefly climbed above $80,000 after the Democrats took control of the US Congress in the midterm elections, then gave back those gains as tech stocks sold off. By Friday, it fell to $74,762, its lowest level since August and remains in the doldrums at the time of writing.

Despite the selloff, Moss points to the reaction to the $6.3 billion Binance hack as a sign that sellers were running out.

Bitcoin fell to $76,320 after the breach and slid to $74,762 on Friday, erasing its post-election gains. It fell further to about $73,500 late Sunday, its lowest level since August, after Nvidia’s board announced it would investigate reports that CEO Jensen Huang had been targeted by a foreign intelligence effort.

Bitcoin found another bottom after reports of Nvidia CEO’s scandal. Source: CoinMarketCap

Flows into US spot Bitcoin ETFs swung sharply during election week. The funds took in about $75 million on Wednesday, then shed $430 million on Thursday.

History is on the side of hodlers however. Bitcoin gained an average of about 53% in the 12 months after previous US midterms, Moss says, using data going back to 2014. That leaves three completed election cycles in his sample. He says he expects Bitcoin to set a new all-time high in 2027.

Moss thinks the Bitcoin treasury company to own is Strategy (MSTR), but believes that holding Bitcoin yourself is still superior. Strategy began buying Bitcoin in 2020 and turned those purchases into a core part of its business. It has used stock and debt sales to fund its purchases, giving investors a way to bet on Bitcoin through a publicly traded company.

By Nov. 2, Strategy held 849,327 BTC, more than 4% of Bitcoin’s maximum supply.

“I don’t think there’s another publicly traded company that will outperform Strategy over the next ten years,” Moss says.

Strategy has the world’s largest corporate Bitcoin trove. Source: BitcoinTreasuries.Net

Leverage can help a Bitcoin treasury company’s shares outperform the cryptocurrency it holds when its price rises.

But even if Strategy outperforms, Moss wouldn’t trade his Bitcoin holdings for its shares.

Related: 1inch lawyer sees agencies driving US crypto regulation as midterms unfold

Before Moss bought his first Bitcoin, he made a living buying foreclosed properties, fixing them up and selling them. He moved into property development, then built and sold a medical equipment business. He says he put the proceeds into Southern California real estate.

When the market crashed in 2008, he lost it all.

“The reward of being right is not worth the risk of getting it wrong,” he says.

Even with his outlook for Strategy, Moss says he’d keep his Bitcoin and use only a small share of his portfolio to pursue higher returns through treasury stocks.

In Moss’s model, Bitcoin could reach $1 million under certain circumstances, when it competes with stocks, bonds, gold and real estate for a considerable share of investors’ wealth. The model estimates how much those assets are worth, projects how that pool could grow as the global money supply expands and calculates a Bitcoin price based on the share it captures.

Financial advisers could help bring in that money via one client’s portfolio at a time, encouraging them to shift a few percentage points out of stocks or bonds and into Bitcoin. The scenario depends on a critical mass of clients doing that, to the point where all those small allocations add up.

The model also assumes Bitcoin stays secure and decentralized. Asked what could derail the scenario, Moss pointed to a failure that compromised either feature.

Moss says $1 million is possible by 2030... though hodler’s might have to wait until 2032.

#5002. Bitcoin Falls as Hot US Inflation Fuels Fears of Bigger Fed Rate Hike

Bitcoin Falls as Hot US Inflation Fuels Fears of Bigger Fed Rate Hike

Bitcoin fell below $74,000 on Tuesday after U.S. inflation came in hotter than expected for the third consecutive month, adding fresh pressure to a crypto market already struggling with falling prices and investor withdrawals.

The Consumer Price Index rose 0.4% in October, above economists' forecasts of 0.3%, according to data released by the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Core CPI, which strips out food and energy prices, climbed 0.3% against expectations of 0.2%, matching September's reading for the third consecutive month.

Headline inflation actually slowed from September's 0.6% increase, but that offered little relief to traders who had expected a bigger slowdown. Both figures exceeded forecasts by 0.1 percentage points, giving the Federal Reserve another reason to keep interest rates elevated.

Bitcoin was trading near $74,200 before the announcement and briefly dropped to approximately $73,525 immediately afterward. The cryptocurrency recovered some ground toward $74,000 but remained under pressure, trading roughly 1% lower over 24 hours.

Ethereum also weakened, slipping toward $2,040 during the initial selloff, while other major altcoins faced selling pressure. Approximately $178 million in crypto long positions were liquidated within an hour of the announcement.

Liquidations happen when exchanges automatically close trades made with borrowed money because their owners no longer have enough collateral to cover potential losses. Those forced sales can push prices even lower, triggering further liquidations.

Bitcoin was already having a difficult week. The asset had lost roughly 4.5% over seven days, while U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded another $201 million in net withdrawals on Monday, extending the selling pressure that preceded the inflation report.

Higher inflation usually means higher interest rates, and higher interest rates tend to be bad news for crypto. When government bonds and cash offer attractive returns, investors have less incentive to hold volatile assets such as Bitcoin, which generate no interest simply by being held.

Higher borrowing costs also reduce the availability of cheap money for speculative investments. That's why Bitcoin, despite its reputation as an inflation hedge, frequently falls when inflation data suggests the Fed will tighten monetary policy.

The latest report landed just hours after Fed Governor Christopher Waller made unusually aggressive comments about the central bank's next move.

Speaking in London, Waller said he would support a quarter-percentage-point interest rate increase in December even if inflation improved. But he also warned that another core CPI reading of 0.3% could justify a larger move.

“I believe a 50 basis point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting,” he said.

October's reading hit precisely that threshold. According to Reuters, financial analysts increased their estimated probability of a 50-basis-point hike to nearly 15% by midmorning, although a smaller quarter-point increase remained the overwhelming favorite.

For context, the Fed currently holds its benchmark interest rate between 3.75% and 4%. A 50-basis-point increase would lift that range to 4.25%-4.50%, making borrowing more expensive across all of the economy.

Bond markets reacted accordingly. The two-year Treasury yield, which closely tracks expectations for Fed policy, posted its biggest increase since April 2025, while the dollar strengthened and U.S. stocks opened lower.

October's inflation report offered a mixed picture of the American economy. Food prices rose 0.5%, energy increased 0.7%, and shelter costs climbed 0.3%, accounting for more than 30% of the monthly headline increase.

Energy inflation slowed considerably from September's 5.2% surge, but price increases remained widespread across services. Recreation costs climbed 0.8%, motor vehicle insurance rose 0.7%, and medical care increased 0.3%.

The report also came with an unusual limitation: The government did not publish its standard annual inflation rate because the 2025 federal shutdown prevented the collection of October data that year. September's last available annual headline reading stood at 3.7%, well above the Fed's 2% inflation target, which is formally measured using a separate index known as Personal Consumption Expenditures.

Crypto investors have seen this pattern before. In October, Bitcoin fell after September's hotter-than-expected inflation report, and the selloff continued as rate-hike expectations strengthened.

This time, the market enters the next Fed meeting with Bitcoin substantially below its October levels and institutional demand showing signs of weakness. Higher Treasury yields could make a sustained recovery more difficult unless inflation cools or investors regain their appetite for risk.

The Fed's final policy meeting of 2026 is scheduled for December 8-9. The next CPI report will arrive on December 10, meaning policymakers will have to make their December rate decision without seeing November's consumer inflation figures.

#4997. Goldman, Citi and Global Banks Commit $270 Million to Challenge Crypto’s Stablecoin Giants

Goldman, Citi and Global Banks Commit $270 Million to Challenge Crypto’s Stablecoin Giants

The group of global banks building a joint stablecoin has put real money on the table, committing around $270 million to fund the venture as it moves from blueprint to buildout.

Goldman Sachs, Citigroup, Banco Santander, and Deutsche Bank are among the institutions backing the effort, Bloomberg reported Tuesday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The capital is meant to cover five years of operations for a planned U.S.-incorporated company that will issue a dollar-denominated stablecoin. Known among participants as Project Faro, the venture is still searching for a chief executive, and its final name remains under discussion.

A spokesperson for the project declined to comment, as did Santander and Deutsche Bank, while Goldman and Citi referred questions to the project.

The funding is the concrete next step for an initiative that first went public late last year. As Decrypt previously reported, 21 of the world’s largest financial institutions committed in September to form a company to issue a U.S. dollar stablecoin, targeting a launch in the first half of 2027, with a euro version and other currencies to follow.

That consortium had more than doubled from an initial group of 10 banks that began exploring the idea in October 2025, and now spans North America, Europe, East Asia, the Middle East and Africa.

Beyond the four named by Bloomberg, its participants include Bank of America, Wells Fargo, Fidelity Investments, PNC, Capital One, TD Bank, UBS, BBVA, Deutsche Bank, Lloyds Banking Group and MUFG, among others. JPMorgan, notably, is not on the list.

The venture is one of several collaborative pushes by established finance into a business long dominated by crypto-native players. Visa, Mastercard and Stripe are among more than 100 companies backing the issuer Open Standard, while European banks have formed Qivalis to issue a euro-denominated coin.

At the moment, Tether’s USDT and Circle’s USDC together account for about 85% of a market worth roughly $290 billion, nearly all of it dollar-pegged. When the bank consortium first went public in September, Circle’s stock fell about 6% as investors priced in the prospect of bank-backed competition for USDC.

The banks are betting that institutional trust, regulatory alignment and existing client networks can win them a share of a settlement business that promises round-the-clock, cross-border payments at lower cost than traditional rails.

The token is being designed to comply with the U.S. GENIUS Act, which created the first federal framework for dollar-pegged tokens in 2025, and, where applicable, the EU’s MiCA regime. The group has said it wants the stablecoin used across wholesale, institutional and retail markets.

For now, Project Faro remains pre-launch, with no issued token, no named CEO, not even a final name, and a 2027 target that leaves ample room for delay.

But the $270 million commitment marks a shift, and a concrete signal that this bank consortium intends to compete for the onchain dollar rather than leave it to crypto.

#4995. Poland’s lower house backs sanctions bill covering crypto freezes

The bill is now before the Senate and would broaden the grounds for adding people and companies to Poland’s national sanctions list.

Poland’s lower house passed a bill that would broaden the country’s sanctions framework and explicitly include crypto assets among the funds authorities can freeze when imposing sanctions.

The Sejm approved the government-backed Act on Restrictive Measures on Friday by 240 votes to 202, with one abstention, and forwarded it to the Senate on Monday, according to official parliamentary records. The bill explicitly includes crypto assets, as defined under the European Union’s Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA), among financial funds that authorities could freeze when imposing sanctions.

Poland’s interior minister would oversee the national sanctions list, with sanctions-listing decisions taking effect the day after authorities publish a designation. Affected individuals and companies could challenge the decision in administrative court, but the bill allows enforcement before a court ruling.

Poland already maintains a national sanctions list under a 2022 law aimed at countering support for Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. The bill would expand the grounds for imposing national sanctions to include certain foreign interference and information manipulation that pose a serious threat to national security.

The Senate has 30 days to approve, amend or reject the legislation. If it takes no action, Poland’s Constitution treats the bill as approved in the form passed by the Sejm, allowing it to proceed to the president for consideration.

November has historically been BTC's strongest month, with an average return of 34.4% and a median gain of 8.6% since 2010.

A hotter-than-expected U.S. inflation report sent crypto prices lower Tuesday, with bitcoin falling below $74,000 as analysts warned of a potential slide below the $70,000 level.

October's Consumer Price Index rose 0.4%, above forecasts of 0.3%, while core CPI increased 0.3% against expectations of 0.2%, raising concerns about the Federal Reserve's interest rate outlook.

"For Bitcoin, higher yields and a stronger dollar could add to recent selling pressure," said Bitget analyst Lewis Huang. "The 200-day moving average near $73,200 is a key support level to watch. A sustained move below it could increase the risk of a deeper pullback."

Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) was trading at $73,363, down 1.6%. The $73,000 level is particularly important, according to Matt Mena, senior crypto research strategist at 21Shares, as it sits near bitcoin's pre-election 2024 all-time high and its April 2025 lows.

A sustained break below that level could send BTC toward $69,000, which would put the cryptocurrency near its 2021 cycle high, Mena said. He also pointed to the 50-week moving average as an important technical support level, warning that a break could put further pressure on Ethereum's $2,000 support, with $1,800 the next level to watch.

Ether (ETH) was down 3.7% to $2,042, while Solana (SOL) dropped more than 4% to trade below $83.

There is still a potential upside scenario if bitcoin can hold the $73,000 level and equities continue to rebound. 21Shares sees BTC potentially retesting $78,000, with a move above that level putting $85,000 in reach by year-end. November has historically been BTC's strongest month, with an average return of 34.4% and a median gain of 8.6% since 2010.

Beyond the immediate macroeconomic pressure, institutional participation and the growing use of derivatives could also influence bitcoin's volatility, according to Wincent senior director Paul Howard.

"As institutional participation in digital assets continues to mature, the growing use of derivatives and sophisticated hedging strategies is helping to reshape Bitcoin's volatility profile," Howard said. "Institutional investors are increasingly able to manage their exposure through options and other risk-management instruments rather than responding to every macroeconomic development by buying or selling the underlying asset."

Without a meaningful shift in monetary policy, regulatory clarity, or institutional capital flows, Howard said, bitcoin is more likely to experience a gradual downward drift than mount a sustained rally toward the $100,000 level previously anticipated.

#4993. This Next-Generation Retinol Has All the Same Benefits But is Gentler on Skin

Here’s all you need to know before adding to cart.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

There are only a handful of skincare ingredients proven to work—and retinol is one of them. It’s part of a larger category of ingredients called retinoids, all derived from vitamin A and considered the gold standard for transforming skin’s texture, clarity, tone, and overall appearance.

There’s a major caveat, though: Because it can be irritating, retinol can’t be used by everyone and you have to be very careful when combining it with other ingredients like niacinamide, vitamin C, and certain exfoliating acids. Whether you have sensitive, acne-prone, or even normal skin, holy-grail retinol can be decidedly less holy if it leaves your complexion red, dry, peeling, or flaky (collectively known as the dreaded ‘retinol purge’).

It’s not just retinol, BTW. All forms of vitamin A, even low-strength versions, have the potential to cause some degree of irritation, especially for newbies taking the plunge (most derms recommend beginning with a lower concentration of the ingredient a few times a week). That’s because retinoids essentially ask skin cells to behave differently by influencing cell turnover, differentiation, and collagen production to improve texture, fine lines, and photoaging, explains Santa Monica-, and Beverly Hills-based board-certified dermatologist Ava Shamban, MD. “In the beginning when using them, there’s an adjustment period, called retinization, that temporarily disrupts the skin barrier, increasing dryness, redness, flaking, and sensitivity, or, as some people call it, the retinoid uglies.” As skin adjusts to the ingredient, these side effects usually taper off and irritation improves.

Meet the experts: Ava Shamban, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist based in Santa Monica and Beverly Hills. Melissa Fox is a celebrity master esthetician and Founder of Flawless by Melissa Fox in Miami and West Palm Beach. Debra Luftman, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist at Schweiger Dermatology in Beverly Hills.

Still, some skin types, especially those with compromised skin barriers or dry, sensitive, eczema- or rosacea-prone skin simply can’t tolerate vitamin A-derived ingredients, no matter how low and slow they go. For that reason, these ingredients have earned an unfair reputation as overly irritating, claims Melissa Fox, a celebrity master esthetician and Founder of Flawless by Melissa Fox in Miami and West Palm Beach. “But you can have a powerful vitamin A treatment without feeling like your skin has to suffer for results.”

That’s because there’s a little-known gentler version of retinol that’s just starting to pop up in over-the-counter products. Known as hydroxypinacolone retinoate (HPR), this next-generation retinoic acid ester is a virtually less-irritating molecule and just may be the secret for those that have been forced to swear off retinol.

Intrigued, I spoke with two dermatologists and an aesthetician, all well-versed in the world of retinoids, about what makes HPR different yet beneficial, and everything you need to know before making it your new favorite skincare ingredient.

So what is this magical molecule? HPR, also listed as ‘granactive retinoid’ on some ingredient labels, is the newest member of the retinoid family and is quickly becoming a go-to alternative to stronger, harsher iterations. “It’s a newer retinoic acid ester that may provide some of the anti-aging benefits of traditional retinoids just with less irritation,” says Debra Luftman, MD, a board-certified dermatologist at Schweiger Dermatology in Beverly Hills.

For traditional retinol to work its magic, it must first convert into retinaldehyde and then into retinoic acid, Dr. Shamban explains. For example, tretinoin, available only by prescription, is already retinoic acid and therefore considered your strongest option. Similarly, HPR comes straight out of the bottle as an already accessible version that doesn’t require any conversions. “This multi-step conversion process is typically what causes the significant discomfort and reaction process,” she adds. Essentially, fewer conversion steps equals less irritation. (But Dr. Shamban is quick to note that irritation also depends on the molecule's biological activity, concentration, delivery system, skin barrier condition, and level of sensitivity.)

Fox adds that HPR is particularly promising for anyone who want the benefits of vitamin A, such as smoother texture, improved appearance of fine lines, and more even-looking skin, but has struggled with traditional formulas. “Early research suggests it may offer good tolerability, which is exciting, but the clinical evidence is still developing, so I wouldn’t call it completely irritation-free.” One study shows that HPR demonstrates well-tolerated anti-aging effects as a gentler option, especially for sensitive skin.

What remains to be seen with any decisive clarity is however HPR compares results-wise with tried-and-true versions of vitamin A like retinol or commonly-prescribed adapalene or tretinoin. While it shows promising activity, Dr. Luftman says there isn't enough clinical evidence to rank it against retinol, retinaldehyde, or prescription tretinoin, which remains most studied.

So why would a skincare brand choose HPR over retinol, with decades of evidence supporting its benefits? From a formulation standpoint, there are several reasons, suggests Dr. Shamban. HPR is oil-soluble and can be incorporated into cosmetically-elegant delivery systems, including emollient-based serums. “The finished product is what actually goes on the skin,” she says, so the nicer to use, the more likely you are to use it. On the other hand, formulating with traditional retinol can be challenging. “It’s sensitive to light, oxygen, and other destabilizing conditions, so HPR may offer those practical formulation advantages.”

Working HPR into your skincare routine isn’t hard, and it follows many of the same rules as traditional retinoids. When considering adding it to your regimen, start gradually. Dr. Luftman recommends applying a small amount to clean, dry skin about three nights per week. “Slowly increase the frequency as your skin adjusts—consistency matters more than concentration.”

Moisturizer is also a must. “You can try the popular sandwich method, which involves applying your cream before and after HPR, to help improve comfort,” suggests Dr. Shaman, “or just apply it after.” She recommends looking for a moisturizer that contains barrier-supporting ingredients, such as ceramides, glycerin, or hyaluronic acid. “You’ll also want to avoid using strong exfoliating acids on the same evening during the introductory phase.” However, it’s perfectly safe to use HPR alongside niacinamide, peptides, or retinol alternatives like bakuchiol.

Like retinol, always use HPR with a daily broad-spectrum sunscreen that has an SPF of at least 30 in the morning, since the ingredient can make the skin more sensitive to the sun.

Elizabeth Arden Retinol + HPR Ceramide Capsules Rapid Skin Renewing Serum

Who doesn’t love a single-use ampoule? And when it contains HPR, retinol, and ceramides, all the better. This gentle formula goes hard to work to reduce the visible appearance of wrinkles in seven days, without the irritation or dryness that retinol can leave behind. Instead, it improves the skin’s texture and tone without any redness or flaking.

The Ordinary Granactive Retinoid 2% Emulsion

A more affordable option worth considering is this serum, which hits on the common signs of aging and uneven tone and texture. Dr. Luftman particularly likes that the product contains HPR in a moisturizing squalane base, so it’s appealing for those with dry or sensitive skin who have difficulty tolerating conventional retinol.

Dry skin and retinol can, unfortunately, go hand in hand. While skincare experts often suggest applying a moisturizer after retinol, why not make life easy and ditch the extra step? This lightweight oil is powered by HPR, four hyaluronic acid derivatives, peptides, and skin-friendly oils to support the skin barrier and deliver a dose of moisture where its needed most. We also like how the oil creates a natural glow and a more refined complexion by morning that improves with each use.

HPR isn’t exclusive to just the face. In fact, it’s making its way into body lotions and serums for the same reason it’s becoming the ingredient du jour in skin care. This potent-yet-gentle lightweight retinoid serum repairs the skin and leaves it better hydrated, silky soft, and smooth.

The delicate skin around the eyes is often also home to fine lines, wrinkles, thinning skin, and discoloration, which is why an eye cream with HPR is a must. Formulated specifically for the eye area, this sensitive skin-approved cream also counts peptides and plant extracts among its hero ingredients to reverse the appearance of tired, aging eyes.

There’s a lot of skin-boosting potential with HPR, but before you slather it all over, read the ingredient label, since some products also contain retinol or retinoids. While HPR can absolutely serve as the sole retinoid in your skincare routine, or be paired with other vitamin A derivatives, Dr. Luftman suggests anyone with sensitive skin use it alone to minimize potential irritation. “There’s sufficient evidence that combining HPR with a retinoid does produce better results,” she adds.

And bear in mind that just like a retinoid, HPR takes weeks for the skin to adjust and for results to show, so patience is a virtue. Your skin may be dry initially, but Dr. Shamban says persistent burning, significant redness, or peeling is a sign to stop and reassess, not push through.

Bottom line: If you’ve tried what seems like every retinol and retinoid out there and still haven’t found the perfect one that doesn’t leave your skin dry, red, and irritated, then it may be time to give HPR a try. Don’t expect an overnight miracle, but you may notice significantly less irritation, making it easier to tolerate. Ultimately, Dr. Shamban says the most effective retinoid is the one that delivers results—and that your skin can tolerate.

In short, the product you’ll actually use.

#4991. Grayscale Bets $1 Million on Zcash as Privacy Coin Tumbles

The asset manager said Tuesday that it plans to direct $1 million to nonprofits and initiatives that support the Zcash ecosystem. It has already awarded $100,000 each to four groups: the Zcash Foundation, policy group Pretty Good Policy for Zcash, Swiss-based Shielded Labs and Project Tachyon, a scaling effort led by one of Zcash’s co-founders.

The funding comes from fees on The Zcash ETF, which trades under the ticker ZCSH. Grayscale said it intends to reinvest all of the sponsor fees it collects from the fund, for up to its first year as an exchange-traded product, into developing and marketing the network and educating people about it.

The commitment is voluntary, and Grayscale can change or end it at any time.

Grayscale also launched Zcash Invest, an educational website and social media presence, along with a national ad campaign, “Zcash: The Next Era of Crypto,” which kicked off with a Wall Street Journal wrap. The ads will run in print, outdoor, digital, streaming and social channels, featuring a zebra motif drawn from the Zcash community.

Competition is building, too, with another Zcash ETF in the works from the Winklevoss twins and Europe’s first Zcash exchange-traded product already launched.

Grayscale’s recent push, though, lands during a rough stretch for Zcash. ZEC fell 11.3% over 24 hours to about $1,043 on Tuesday after briefly dipping below $1,000, according to CoinGecko.

It’s down roughly 19% over 30 days and well off its October highs above $1,500, though still up about 75% from a year ago. Perpetual futures open interest stands at about $2.3 billion, and Zcash previously tumbled after leverage built up during its rally.

#4989. Forward Industries grows SOL treasury to 8.7 million as token hits nearly three-month low

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Forward secured a $50 million credit line and committed up to $50 million to a tokenized U.S. home-price product.

Forward Industries added roughly 226,000 SOL to its treasury through purchases and staking rewards since the end of September.

The company said Tuesday that its holdings reached approximately 8.7 million SOL, worth roughly $726.5 million, totaling about 1.5% of Solana's total circulating supply.

Forward controls the largest Solana treasury among publicly traded companies by a wide margin, with its closest rival, DeFi Development Corp., holding just over 2.6 million SOL.

The update comes amid a slide in SOL and Forward's own shares Forward Industries, Inc. (FWDI). SOL dropped as low as $82 on Tuesday, its lowest level in almost three months, before recovering to around $83.50. Forward shares also dipped to a near three-month low of $5.25, leaving the stock down more than 28% so far this year.

Forward also disclosed that it had secured a two-year, $50 million credit facility from an unnamed U.S. national bank. The loan is backed by SOL that will remain staked, which will allow Forward to continue earning rewards.

The company added that it committed up to $50 million to USHP, a tokenized product that tracks U.S. home prices whose launch was also anchored by the Galaxy-Sharplink Onchain Yield Fund. It plans to use income from the investment to purchase more SOL.

#4988. When the Mountain Follows You Home: What Recovery Looks Like for Outdoor Accident Survivors

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

How a new wave of mental health professionals is helping people confront the trauma that can linger long after they make it back.

ON A LATE fall day in 2023, Laura Stilwell and her boyfriend, Marcus Wybrow, were descending the long mountain trail back to their vehicle after a day of ice-climbing among the rugged peaks west of Calgary, Alberta.

Wybrow, 29, was close to reaching full certification as an alpine guide. Stilwell, 27, had some backcountry experience, but this was her first time ice-climbing. They hadn’t attempted anything most climbers would consider difficult or risky. The point was simply to be in the mountains together, and for Wybrow to introduce Stilwell to a sport he loved.

Avalanche Canada, the main organization issuing the public avalanche forecasts that backcountry enthusiasts check before heading out, wasn’t yet forecasting for the season. (It was too early in the season to warrant forecasts.) Though there wasn’t much snow along the couple’s route, the pair carried avalanche gear to be safe.”

Then, as they hiked toward the trailhead, the snow suddenly cracked off the slope above and slid fast, overwhelming them in white chaos. The avalanche mostly buried Stilwell and fully engulfed Wybrow. It threw Stilwell onto her left side; her right hand was the only part of her body that remained unburied. With that hand, she was able to clear the snow from her face so she could breathe—then started digging herself out, screaming. She isn’t sure how long it took to free herself, but it felt like an eternity.

But this story isn’t about that horrific day. It’s about what happens after.

MANY SURVIVORS OF accidents—climbing falls, near drownings, mountain bike crashes, exposure, even near misses while hiking or trail running—suffer from post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD). Other mental and emotional injuries unique to outdoor accidents abound in these individuals too, including loss of identity and a severed connection to the natural world that once brought feelings of awe and peace. That’s all on top of anxiety, depression, and grief.

Yet we often don’t hear about this terrible aftermath, or how those suffering might find a way through it.

That’s because most narratives about adventure sport accidents tend to deal with the incident itself—the factors, both human and natural, that led up to it, and what it took to survive—while shying away from the psychological impact on those involved.

This is partly due to a culture at large that struggles to deal with mental health issues in general, combined with an outdoor one that has historically prized independence, stoicism, and strength. “Many of us learned how to disconnect from ourselves in order to respond to an emergency, but very few of us were ever taught how to reconnect once the emergency was over,” says Jenny Fiebig, a licensed clinical professional counselor and one of the first therapists to specialize in outdoor trauma.

At the same time, shockingly little access to mental health resources exists in the small mountain towns that are the hubs for these sports—despite the fact many of these areas have exploded in population since the pandemic, as has participation in the activities.

Climbing, ski touring, surfing, kayaking, and trail running are all among the fastest-growing sports in the country. But while many therapists are well trained in trauma, says Fiebig, they’re not always trained in outdoor culture. “If someone doesn’t understand why a person would willingly return to a landscape where they experienced trauma, or put themselves out there in the first place, it’s easy to misinterpret that decision as avoidance, denial, or unnecessary risk.”

The result is that those struggling with this complex trauma have had almost nowhere to turn, leaving a rapidly increasing subset of people isolated in silence. Until now.

Passionate recreationalists and mental health professionals are beginning to address that gap and offer alternative modalities to help outdoor accident survivors heal and to support victims’ families and communities as well. Some of these modalities are new, like peer-support groups and somatic nervous system exercises that help survivors reconnect with nature as part of the healing process. Others, like one-on-one talk therapy, are not revolutionary, but they’ve been dramatically underused for healing in a community that so desperately needs it.

Previously, the response to trauma in mountain towns was largely along the lines of “Let’s go drink a beer and forget about it,” says Fiebig, who works with Colorado-based Survivors of Outdoor Adventures and Recovery (SOAR), one of the organizations leading this movement that has nationwide reach and is growing. “Ignoring the signs or symptoms of PTSD affects relationships, jobs, marriages. It becomes systemic. There’s a high suicide rate in mountain towns, a high depression rate—mental health is a huge issue.”

In adventure culture, “we weren’t supposed to talk about our traumas,” says Darcy Chenoweth, DNP, a 49-year-old psychiatric nurse practitioner in Missoula, Montana, who’s also a mountaineer, climber, and skier. Chenoweth is on the board of directors for Canmore, Alberta–based Mountain Muskox, another leading organization in the field, for which she coauthored a paper on care approaches to trauma and loss in the mountains.

Once, when Chenoweth was talking through her own outdoor trauma with a friend with whom she ice-climbed, he told her she “was just being highly sensitive.”

This new movement is different from anything that came before because most of the professionals involved have been through outdoor trauma themselves, and they’ve seen firsthand how the care gap leaves survivors adrift in the hellish experiences that come after disaster. Also, given that adventure sports and the outdoor arena have historically been male-dominated—and traditionally masculine traits like speed, capability, and infallible expertise have been valued, while showing vulnerability was often seen as weakness—perhaps it’s no surprise that this movement is almost entirely women-led.

And for Laura Stilwell, it couldn’t have come soon enough.

AFTER THAT DAY in the mountains, Stilwell descended into a nightmare of grief tangled up with trauma, an emotional storm that many adventure accident survivors experience. She couldn’t sleep. When she woke in the predawn, she’d think, It’s too early, I can’t make it through a day this long. In the immediate aftermath, she was “a numb ghost, feeling nothing, not really living,” she says.

Then the pain roared in. Anything that sparked a memory of Wybrow, from books to clothing to photos, sent her into a sudden breakdown. Sometimes her grief wailed louder, the trauma manifesting in nightmares and hypervigilance. She’d lived in Squamish, British Columbia, for only a year at that point; her community was still fairly small and couldn’t identify with what she was going through.

Most of her friends were newer to adventure sports, “without a grasp of how bad things can be,” she says. “I was in that camp too, and then this happened to me, and it’s like that innocence just gets ripped away.”

Stilwell also grappled with thoughts of what she could have done differently that day, and battled with blame, shame, and guilt—another unique layer of trauma that outdoor accident survivors frequently suffer.

Many of these activities require an enormous amount of knowledge and skill, from snow science or river hydraulics to understanding weather, navigating remote terrain, operating technical equipment, performing companion rescue, and accepting inherent risk. An accident is often treated as a gap in that knowledge—a mistake of sometimes fatal proportions for which judgment, both from the self and others, is often lobbed liberally.

Consider, for example, the judgment-laden media coverage of the February 2026 avalanche near Lake Tahoe in Northern California that claimed nine lives, six of them mothers.

“Folks who choose to connect to outdoor spaces through recreation often experience a joy and connection that is hard to find in our manufactured way of life,” says Fiebig. “But when we put ourselves in the ‘risk’ of being outside, we also get shamed when things go wrong—a sort of ‘well, why did you choose to be there?’”

Chenoweth says that the rise of social media, in addition to glorifying adventure without acknowledging the social and emotional costs, only exacerbates the situation. These platforms allow “many people outside the community [to] live vicariously through the activities of mountain athletes. This often leads to outside judgment from those with no knowledge of the individuals involved in an incident or its context,” she writes in her coauthored paper.

The adventure community itself can also contribute to the shame survivors feel by scrutinizing the accident for mistakes. This is partly to learn from the incident, and partly a defense tactic to distance ourselves from the trauma.

“There’s a culture of analyzing all the data after an accident that takes the humanity out of the survivors,” says Fiebig. This scrutiny, she says, may send survivors into a “shame spiral.” “We need to know what happened, but we also need to make that space for the survivor to feel cared for, to feel validated that they did the best they could do, and recognize the pain that they just went through.”

Trying to navigate her own mental health, Stilwell did one-on-one therapy. It was a lifeline, but when a session would end, she’d panic knowing that she had to get through an entire week before the next session. She’d been a digital marketer, but she couldn’t work. She couldn’t focus, engage with other people, or regulate her emotions enough for a professional setting. She started watching television for up to 10 hours a day as a survival mechanism to distract herself from the pain, even while contemplating not being alive anymore at all.

SOME MAY STILL question why anyone would venture into the outdoors knowing that these pursuits might deliver such trauma and loss.

Science offers answers. Even just 20 minutes per day spent in nature can lower stress hormone levels, boost mental health, and increase life expectancy, and spending time outdoors provides an added sense of well-being specifically for women, per research. Time in nature serves as an escape from the everyday pressures unique to females (such as conforming to expectations about weight, appearance, and demeanor), according to an REI study on women in the outdoors.

But many recreationalists say it’s about more than what the research shows. The very presence of risk is part of what makes adventure so alluring and often so hard to describe to those outside of the community. Navigating situations with risk, where individual decisions, grit, and capability can determine personal fate, demands the kind of complete focus that some try to achieve through meditation practice—and is often impossible to find in our busy world.

Navigating those situations in the company of others can also be a profound experience. “I have built the strongest, deepest, most fulfilling relationships in my life while doing activities like climbing or ski touring,” Stilwell says. “You put yourself in a situation where there’s some risk involved, [along with] decision-making and fear, you have to manage your nervous system—it allows you to connect with somebody so much deeper, to get to know what motivates them, what scares them, what exhilarates them. And I feel so much more seen in those environments.”

Then there’s the awe factor. Awe, “our response to powerful things that are obscure, vast, and mysterious,” can have benefits from “calming inflammation to activating the vagus nerve, deactivating our brain’s stress center, to reducing pain perception,” according to Dacher Keltner, PhD, author of Awe: The New Science of Everyday Wonder and How It Can Transform Your Life. Awe often helps us transcend the self so that even our perception of time changes and we feel part of something bigger. Many people who love the outdoors will say, in one way or another, that connecting to this feeling through recreation might be the closest they come to grace.

But, says Chenoweth, “awe and tragedy are not separate. Both push us beyond ourselves, where the ideas we have about the world shift, and sometimes shatter, and human consciousness confronts the unknown. You can’t say, ‘I’ll choose awe, and avoid tragedy.’ The unknown holds both.”

STILWELL’S THERAPIST EVENTUALLY recommended that she check out SOAR.

Starr Jamison, an outdoor professional in Telluride, Colorado, founded SOAR after losing three friends to separate avalanches in a single year and wading through her own grief and traumatic experiences. She also watched people involved in or adjacent to accidents attempt or commit suicide after losing loved ones—including after a highly publicized avalanche in October 2017 on Montana’s Imp Peak that claimed the life of 23-year-old Inge Perkins, a rising star in the backcountry skiing and climbing worlds.

Perkins’s partner and famed climber Hayden Kennedy, 27, survived the slide but couldn’t locate Perkins, who was buried with her transceiver turned off. After searching in vain for hours, Kennedy drove home to their apartment in Bozeman, wrote a note describing where to search for her body, and then took his own life.

That tragedy spurred a reeling mountain community to reckon with unaddressed trauma. Organizations formed to support the professional community of guides, ski patrollers, wildland firefighters, and others who are outdoors for their jobs, including Responder Alliance, run by Laura McGladrey, and Climbing Grief Fund, started by Madaleine Sorkin.

But Jamison found that support for people who simply wanted to adventure for the love of it (rather than it being their job) remained nonexistent. Eventually she was connected to a couple of mental health professionals who were also considering how to bridge this gap, including Fiebig. SOAR piloted its first peer-support group for outdoor trauma survivors in 2021.

The organization now offers community connection workshops on topics such as mindfulness, survivor’s guilt, and neuroplastic healing. It also maintains a growing public list of counselors, therapists, body workers, psychiatric nurse practitioners, and resiliency coaches who speak the language of outdoor trauma and grief.

SOAR’s peer-support model is akin to Alcoholics Anonymous, but whereas AA is led by rotating members, these groups are led by professional facilitators. The sessions, which run monthly for five to six months, are free and often take place over Zoom, so they can be as accessible as possible. SOAR’s groups comprise people with common experiences: those who survived an outdoor incident in which another person perished; those who’ve lost a partner but weren’t on the scene; parents who’ve lost children to outdoor trauma; people who’ve sustained injuries in adventures; and others. Stilwell joined a group facilitated by Fiebig specifically for survivors of outdoor accidents in which their partners had died.

Like most of the women leading this movement, Fiebig, 46, follows the wounded healer arc (essentially, helping others as a means of healing yourself). She’s a whitewater boater who experienced several near misses with drowning in her 20s, and she once fell into a Class 5 stretch of river without a life jacket when the bank gave way while she was hiking. She chalked it up to not having enough skills and dismissed the accompanying emotional trauma as invalid, because, after all, she’d survived. It’s a common response, but insidiously dangerous. While not glaringly tragic, such experiences can have serious psychological impacts—and more so when they’re cumulative.

“I never sought help because of the shame of the experience—I felt like I could have prevented it,” Fiebig says. “Especially as a woman in the outdoors, it was easy for me to immediately go to blaming myself for not being good enough.”

Fiebig still grieves the two decades of her life during which she believed she was “not enough” because of what she perceived to be her mistakes, which made her pull back from whitewater even though she loved it. “I want to help marginalized communities work through their traumas so they’re not missing years of their lives, holding themselves back because of what happened.”

This movement, it must be said, is not exclusive to women. Far from it. While most facilitators and movement leaders are women, the people seeking help are split evenly between men and women, says Sarah Hueniken, 52, an alpine guide who cofounded Mountain Muskox, which runs in-person peer-support groups, after losing a good friend to an avalanche at one of her ice-climbing camps. Several men are also on her organization’s board of directors. Jamison, too, reports that SOAR’s workshops and support groups, while fairly balanced, are mostly composed of women.

WHEN STILWELL LOGGED on to her first group session with SOAR, she was apprehensive, wary, and unsure what to expect—which Fiebig says is normal. “I didn’t know whether people would have similar experiences to me or not,” Stilwell says. “I remember asking, ‘Is there space for grief here, or is it focused on trauma?’ ”

Fiebig told her that everyone in the group had lost someone in an accident and had a paired experience of both grief and trauma. Thank God. I found the right place, Stilwell thought.

Fiebig explains that a shared lived experience allows people to talk freely about what happened without feeling the need to hold back in order to protect others in the group. But before anyone is expected to share stories, Fiebig offers some psychoeducation on trauma and grief. She talks about the nervous system, fight-or-flight, dissociation, and other physical and emotional symptoms, which Stilwell called “epiphanies that validated how I was feeling.” Eventually, Fiebig says, members begin taking the group where it needs to go, turning it into “a kind of living space” with Fiebig there to ground and guide.

Peer support has been associated with reduced isolation and improved well-being, and it has helped survivors find an enhanced capacity for meaning after traumatic events, per research. It also provides perspective in introducing people to others at different points in their journeys, offering both validation and hope. And it allows for reciprocity. “Unlike traditional models of care that position individuals primarily as recipients, peer-based approaches enable a more fluid exchange. Individuals who are further along in their process can support those who are earlier, while continuing to engage with their own experience,” write Chenoweth and her fellow authors in their paper.

SOAR is currently training a new cohort of outdoor trauma–informed mental health professionals. And every peer-support group that SOAR and Mountain Muskox run creates new ambassadors who can help the next people in the community suffering from trauma and grief. Thus the movement—like all effective groundswells—is meant to be culturally transformative, changing the way we understand the motivations for connecting to nature through risky endeavors and the way we show care and support for people in the aftermath of the unthinkable.

ALONG WITH ATTENDING the support group, Stilwell is undergoing somatic experiencing therapy to address trauma and stress held in the body and nervous system.

“A lot of my trauma is on my left side, because that’s how I was lying in the snow, and my body remembered that,” Stilwell says. “I found it really hard to lie in bed on my left side. I was immediately anxious, my heart rate went up, my breathing pattern changed.”

Stilwell’s practitioner guided her in small movements and exercises that helped to desensitize her physical responses. Some practitioners, like Brooke Edwards, a resiliency coach who works with SOAR, do visualization and meditation and occasionally even take survivors back to the environments of their trauma to help them reconnect to nature through somatic exercises.

Stilwell might join the next support group that SOAR runs, because the path to healing is not linear. “There’s no end date to this,” she says. “I think Western culture sees grief as something that needs to be healed, not something that needs to be carried. You have to carry it with you your whole life. There’s no healing from it. Somebody who had a huge influence on my life is no longer there. I’ve had to relearn who I am on my own.”

She still has nightmares and wrestles with hypervigilance. She still grapples with whether she had enough experience to be out there that day. “It’s inevitable that you ask yourself, ‘If I had been the strongest, most fit, most practiced, well-trained person, could things have been different?’ I’ll never have the answer to that question. I don’t think the answer is always yes. Ultimately, Mother Nature is more powerful than us.”

But she went back to work in April. And while she’ll never ice-climb again, she says, she’s relearning her passion for playing in the outdoors, where she discovers so much capacity to see beauty and feels connected to Wybrow. He was the one who instilled that love in her. She feels she’s out there for both of them now.

This is also the first time Stilwell has shared her story publicly. It’s daunting, to say the least. “Connecting with people who have been through something similar has been one of the most useful things to me,” she says. “I feel like I’m ready to pay it forward. And if my story could help someone else, then it’s worth sharing. If I hadn’t found some of these resources…I don’t want to say I wouldn’t be here, but it could look very different than it does.”

#4987. Elon Musk Calls Claude Opus 5.5 a 'Masterpiece', Admits Grok 4.8 Won't Match It

Elon Musk Calls Claude Opus 5.5 a 'Masterpiece', Admits Grok 4.8 Won't Match It

Elon Musk has given a rival AI model an unusually enthusiastic endorsement while acknowledging that his own next release won't be able to beat it. Asked how Grok 4.8 would compare with Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5, the xAI founder answered it would be “not as good.”

“Opus 5.5 is a masterpiece,” Musk posted on X.

The admission came in a reply on X Tuesday to a question from a user named Dimdv. Musk added that Grok 5, a next generation model that will be released after this update, “should exceed Opus 5.5,” potentially in two or three months, although he gave further details for that prediction or a firm date for its release—and he’s known for not sticking to his own target dates.

For a company trying to catch up with Anthropic and OpenAI, the distinction matters. Grok 4.8 is the update Musk expects to put in some users' hands this week; Grok 5 is the model he believes could eventually put his company ahead, until competing AI companies release a new model.

The exchange began with a technical update in which Musk said Grok 4.8 had finished pretraining but was still undergoing reinforcement learning, or RL, a phase in which a model's responses are refined using feedback and rewards. He said the team had also spent considerable time cleaning up supervised fine-tuning data, the examples used to teach the model how to follow instructions.

Musk said the company had abandoned JAX, a machine-learning framework, and SGLang, software used to serve AI models, in favor of an inference and reinforcement-learning system rewritten in C and C++. Inference is the process of generating answers after a user sends a prompt; rewriting the underlying software can offer engineers more control over performance, though Musk provided no figures showing the resulting gains.

Developer tetsuo celebrated the announcement with “C wins again,” prompting Musk to clarify that the code was “C with a little C++.” He subsequently credited Starlink engineers with work that helped produce the Grok training and inference stack, pointing to SpaceX's practice of writing its own headers and handling precise time calculations, including leap seconds.

Musk described Grok 4.8 as “not yet incredible” but said its foundations were finally built correctly. He expects a soft rollout to Bot users later this week, giving the company an early test before any broader release.

Anthropic released Opus 5.5 on September 22, delivering a performance comparable to its higher-end Fable 5.1 model on most work while costing 40% less to run than the previous Opus version on typical workloads. The model targets difficult programming jobs and tasks in which AI agents work through multiple steps with limited human assistance.

On Terminal-Bench 4.0, which tests whether AI agents can complete complicated tasks in a command-line environment and scores them by the percentage successfully finished, Anthropic reported 66.4% for Opus 5.5 versus 57.9% for OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra. Grok is very far behind in the leaderboards.

Musk has not published Grok 4.8 scores that would establish how large the gap really is. His immediate target is the limited rollout later this week; the promised Grok 5 challenge to Opus 5.5 is still, by his own estimate, two or three months away.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD falling to $73,518, nearly matching the low it set on Sunday, its lowest level since Aug. 21.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The October print of the Consumer Price Index (CPI) came in at 0.4% month-on-month, above the anticipated 0.3%. CPI, which lacked a year-on-year component due to the absence of data collection during the 2025 US government shutdown, nonetheless showed a cooling rate of growth versus September, when the monthly increase hit 0.6%.

“The index for shelter rose 0.3 percent in October, accounting for over thirty percent of the monthly all items increase. The food index increased 0.5 percent in October, as the food at home index rose 0.4 percent and the food away from home index increased 0.5 percent,” the official release from the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) stated.

Whereas energy price increases had accounted for a major portion of the September CPI gain, October’s energy index advanced significantly less at 0.7% versus the previous 5.2% move.

Economist Robin Brooks described the CPI result as “discouraging” while noting that the oil-price spike had contributed to the September numbers.

“All the details of this CPI report are discouraging, but October was also the peak of the second big oil price shock this year and that’s obviously going to bleed into core CPI inflation,” he wrote in an analysis.

In a speech earlier on Tuesday, Federal Reserve governor Christopher Waller said that he favored hiking interest rates by 25 basis points in December even if inflation cooled from September. Data from CME Group’s FedWatch Tool showed markets pricing in a 96.3% chance of that outcome at the Fed’s December meeting as a result.

Following the CPI report, those odds turned more hawkish, with a 25-basis-point hike attracting 85.3% odds and a 50-basis-point hike attracting 14.7%.

Fed target-rate probability comparison for December FOMC meeting (screenshot). Source: CME Group

“That includes a spike in the equity put/call ratio which jumped over 1.0 and is even higher than during the trade war selloff in April 2025,” it added.

#4983. Near AI Breach Exposes User Details and Data From 18 AI Agents

Near AI Breach Exposes User Details and Data From 18 AI Agents

Near AI has disclosed a breach that exposed account information belonging to about 100 users and data from 18 AI agents after an attacker exploited software running its servers.

The company said in a November 9 report that an attacker used a remote code execution vulnerability in SGLang, an open-source engine that runs AI models and generates responses to user requests.

The exploit allowed the intruder to execute commands on one inference server and then reach other parts of Near AI’s infrastructure.

Investigators traced the intrusion to an internal system connected to Private Chat, a feature the company had already retired. The attacker downloaded names and email addresses for approximately 100 users, along with subscription plan and account balance information for some of them.

Another 18 agents were accessed on an older deployment of Near AI Agents, where the attacker retrieved their chat histories, settings and logs. Those agents were not running inside NEAR AI’s confidential computing environment, which is designed to shield information from the company operating the servers.

Logs also exposed NEAR AI Cloud API keys belonging to two customers. These keys function like digital credentials that applications use to access cloud services; Near AI says it has revoked both, but did not say whether the attacker used them.

Near AI found no indication that stored Private Chat conversations or uploaded files had been accessed. The confirmed theft involved account records and legacy agent data, while exposure of live confidential requests remains a possibility identified by the investigation.

Hardware isolation does not guarantee that the software inside the protected environment is safe. “It does not protect against a vulnerability in the software running inside the environment,” Near AI acknowledged, explaining that the SGLang flaw let the attacker run code where confidential requests were being processed.

SGLang had already attracted scrutiny from security researchers. In July, Carnegie Mellon University’s CERT Coordination Center documented six vulnerabilities in the project, including bugs that could let attackers execute commands or expose credentials, depending on how servers were configured.

Near AI did not identify the specific SGLang flaw exploited in this incident, so there is no basis to link the breach to any of those earlier disclosures. It reported the vulnerability to SGLang’s developers.

Near, to its credit, was quick to fix the issues.

“On detection, we revoked all active sessions and administrative credentials, replaced all agent tokens, took Private Chat offline, and deployed a fix for the SGLang vulnerability across all inference servers,” the project said.

”We've completed the rotation of all remaining credentials and a full forensic review. We have notified users whose account details or agents we have confirmed were accessed and will make any regulatory notifications that are required.”

Near AI notified users whose account records or agents it confirmed were accessed and says it will make any required regulatory notifications. It also warned users to be wary of unsolicited messages referring to their Near AI accounts or balances, and contacted the owners of all 18 affected agents individually.

#4982. Polymarket US trading volume jumps 266% despite regulatory battles

Trading volume on Polymarket’s US exchange surged from June to October, fueled by sports betting, even as the platform faces mounting regulatory scrutiny.

Trading on Polymarket’s US exchange more than tripled between June and October, up about 266% as the company faced state and federal scrutiny.

Monthly volume rose to $14.77 billion from $4.04 billion over that period, according to data from analytics platform TickerTracker, which counts trades at the contracts’ full payout value.

Polymarket told CNBC on Tuesday that its annualized revenue had topped $1.8 billion, up from more than $1 billion in June.

Combo contracts, which bundle multiple predictions into a single wager, accounted for $8.6 billion, or 58.2%, of October’s volume. Sports contracts were the next-largest category at $4.57 billion, or 31%.

The platform’s US arm has grown rapidly since the company widened access in May, as states challenge whether federally regulated prediction markets can offer sports contracts without state gambling licenses. Polymarket began admitting US traders to the separate exchange in late 2025, three years after a settlement with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) led it to block US trading on its international platform.

Polymarket US trading volume by category in October 2026. Source: TickerTracker

The growth also follows a surge in sports-related trading during the FIFA World Cup, which ran from June 11 to July 19. Soccer contracts generated $1.81 billion in trading volume in June, up from $85.4 million in May, according to TickerTracker.

Political markets also saw increased activity surrounding the US midterm elections. Election-related contracts generated $108.2 million in trading volume on Polymarket’s US exchange during the first 10 days of November, compared with $16.6 million throughout September. Despite the increase, political markets accounted for just 2.2% of the platform’s November trading volume.

Meanwhile, Polymarket is pursuing a $1 billion funding round at a reported $21 billion post-money valuation. Investment firm 1789 Capital, where Donald Trump Jr. is a partner, is reportedly leading the round with an expected contribution of about $300 million.

Related: Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

Despite the figures, Polymarket continues to face regulatory scrutiny on several fronts. The CFTC was reportedly investigating Polymarket earlier this year, while US Senators John Curtis and Adam Schiff separately called on the regulator in June to examine allegations that the company paid social media influencers to promote fabricated trades.

Responding to the allegations, the company told Cointelegraph that it was “conducting a comprehensive audit of active promotional content to ensure it complies with our standards, as well as applicable regulatory and legal disclosure requirements.”

Polymarket is also among several prediction market operators facing legal challenges from US states. New York Attorney General Letitia James sued the company in September, alleging that its sports-related event contracts violated state gambling laws. The lawsuit is part of a broader wave of state actions against prediction market platforms, including Kalshi, over whether their contracts fall under federal commodities regulation or state gambling laws.

The CFTC has defended its authority to regulate prediction markets, pursuing legal action involving 10 states over efforts to enforce state gambling laws against the platforms.

The jurisdictional dispute could ultimately reach the US Supreme Court. In September, New Jersey officials petitioned the court to hear their case against Kalshi, seeking a ruling that could clarify which authorities have jurisdiction over sports-related prediction markets.
