#4981. 5 Best Acupressure Pens for Targeted Massage, According to Experts

Here’s how to use one, and what to expert.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

There's a lot coming at you on a daily basis to stress you out. But even though you probably lean into the big de-stressors like exercising regularly, doing breathing exercises on demand, and talk therapy as needed, sometimes you just need a way to dial down the tension building up inside you without visiting a provider's office or hitting the gym. That's where acupressure pens come in.

These handheld tools, which are sometimes called acupuncture pens, are designed to target pressure points in your body to help relieve tension and pain. "Acupressure stimulates acupuncture points without using needles," says Tom Ingegno, DACM, acupuncturist and founder of Charm City Integrative Health. "It lets people take a more active role in their own health without necessarily seeing a licensed [acupuncture] practitioner."

An acupressure pen has a shape that's optimized to stimulate acupuncture points, or areas on the body that are linked to certain health benefits, explains Margie Navarro, DAOM, doctor of acupuncture at Vivacity Healthcare in New York City. "The tool has a rather small head that is smooth and rounded, so that it is a relatively comfortable way to stimulate pressure on a small area or point," she says.

People use these tools to ease anxiety, headaches, and general pain, and there is some data to suggest acupressure in general may be helpful in treating all of these. (Because each acupressure pen is slightly different, it's a little tricky to prove that they'll work for these issues.)

Still, acupuncture experts say these pens are unlikely to hurt if you want to give them a try. "It gives people a way to play an active role in their health," Navarro says. "Even just a sense of agency can improve health outcomes." Sounds like something you can get behind? These acupressure pens can get you started.

There are a ton of acupressure pens out there to choose from, and most can be used all over your body. Odacite's Mon Ami tool was specifically designed with facial acupressure in mind, with a rounded tip on one end and a spatula-scoop hybrid on the other. That allows users to apply products like moisturizers and serums to targeted areas, before massaging it in.

This metal pen feels cool to the touch, creating a soothing feel as you work out muscle tension. It also comes with a pretty pink carry bag to make it easy to tote it with you while you travel. Just be aware that you'll need to clean this regularly, especially if you use it with beauty products.

Most acupressure pens are inexpensive, but the Wellgler pen is a mere $7, making it the cheapest option on our list. The acupressure pen has two different rounded heads, allowing you to really get in there for smaller areas you want to target, like TMJ muscles. But the larger end is also a good option for massaging the rest of your face, and even your sinuses.

This pen feels a little weightier than you'd expect for $7—that can be a pro or a con, depending on your personal preference in an acupressure pen. It also has contoured grips on both ends, so you can get a comfortable, secure grasp while massaging or pressing on any given area.

The big draw of an acupressure pen is its tips—they're what you use to target pressure points, after all. But the Feelfree acupressure pen has a long, tapered tip that you can use to roll over various parts of your body, giving you even more options than "just" acupressure.

The dual rounded ends feature different sizes, so you can target your TMJ muscles with one end, before easing tension in your neck with the other, larger end. It comes with a microfiber cloth for easy cleaning, along with a travel bag, so you can easily do acupressure on the go.

Most acupressure pens you'll find are made of some kind of metal, both for a cooling feel against your skin and for ease of cleaning. The Yutiann acupressure pen is made of wood for a gentler feel. This acupressure pen is relatively large, at 6.1 centimeters, giving you more options for use.

You can use the pen and its length on your hands and face, along with your back and feet. Multiple grooves near the top of the pen create a molded feel for a comfortable grip that you may not get as easily with smaller metal pens. Just be aware that wooden tools aren't as easy to clean as their metal counterparts. This pen's larger size also means the smaller tip may not be petite enough to target teeny tense muscles.

This Rena Chris set gives you a little of everything for working out stress and pain in your face. It features a contoured acupressure pen, along with a gua sha tool, both made of a sturdy stainless steel. So, you can do targeted acupressure, followed by gua sha to ease tension and shuttle away swelling.

This set has a reflexology guide to show various pressure points on your hands, feet, and ears that you may want to touch to soothe whatever ails you. It also comes with a cleaning cloth to make clean-up a breeze. Just be aware that there's no included travel bag, making you on your own for that one.

You have a few options for use with an acupressure pen. In general, these are designed to stimulate pressure points in your body. Most people use acupressure pens for some type of pain relief, whether it's headaches or TMJ discomfort. But these pens can also be used to ease anxiety and stress.

Many acupressure pens are made of medical-grade stainless steel. However, some are made from brass or other metals. You can also find acupressure pens made of wood, although these are less common.

Every pen is slightly different, making it important to read the manufacturer's directions first. But you can typically clean an acupressure pen by wiping it with a soft cloth before you use it.

Acupressure pens shouldn't break your budget, and most cost $25 or less. "The key is the stimulation of the point, not necessarily the tool you're using," Ingegno says. "You don't need an expensive device to practice acupressure, but a pen can make it easier."

There is data to suggest that acupressure can help with easing a range of pain and discomfort. "Manual pressure can have therapeutic benefits in a variety of conditions, including muscular pain," says Navarro. But you need to know which areas to target. And, of course, not everyone will feel pain or stress relief from acupressure, regardless of whether you get it from a pen or a provider.

Ingegno suggests learning about pressure points in the body before using an acupressure pen. "I used to recommend books, but these days there's so much information online that it's pretty easy to find a starting point," he says. Once you've figured out the area you want to target, he suggests applying gentle to moderate pressure for about 30 to 60 seconds. "Some points may feel a little sore or tender when pressed, and that's not necessarily a bad thing, but you shouldn't be causing yourself pain," Ingegno says. "More pressure isn't always better."

You can apply steady pressure on a point or gently rub or massage the area with the tool, according to Ingegno. "Neither approach is wrong, and both can be useful," he says.

There are electronic acupressure pens, which deliver small electrical impulses to pressure points. "Follow the manufacturer's instructions, since there are several different types available and they don't all work the same way," Ingegno says.

Most people can safely use acupressure pens, according to Ingegno. But there are a few caveats. If you're pregnant, he recommends taking a pass. If you're using blood thinners, Ingegno also suggests being gentle, since you may bruise more easily. "Electronic acupressure pens also require a few additional precautions," Ingegno says. "People with pacemakers or other implanted electronic medical devices should avoid electrical stimulation unless their healthcare provider has specifically cleared them."

If you have broken skin or areas where the tissue is already damaged, Navarro recommends avoiding using the acupressure pen on that area.

Nvidia Erases 2026 Gains as Republicans Threaten Jensen Huang With Subpoena

Nvidia shares plunged to $166.95 Tuesday afternoon, leaving the AI chipmaker down 18.42% for November and wiping out all the gains it had accumulated in 2026.

The selloff accelerated as congressional Republicans threatened to subpoena CEO Jensen Huang over his reported relationship with a suspected Chinese intelligence operative.

At that price, Nvidia was roughly 10.5% below its $186.50 closing price at the end of 2025 and 31.4% below its recent $243.37 peak. November is shaping up as its worst month since September 2022, when shares lost roughly 19.6%; the company suffered an even deeper 32% crash in April of that year.

Tuesday's drop exceeded 6% in afternoon trading, after a 4.3% fall on Monday. The two-day decline was on track to erase more than $470 billion in market value. Even after the significant drop, some analysts would still view the stock as extremely overbought in the long term, with prices much higher than what would be considered safe based on technical indicators.

Just for context, even an additional 20% crash (-43% from October) would be considered healthy in an isolated technical analysis, leaving the price still above the average price of the last 50 months. Prices tend to either bounce or trade near that line. When prices go too far away from the average, that usually means there is an unusual appetite for the asset, typically driven more by FOMO than fundamentals.

House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast told ABC News on Tuesday that he was prepared to subpoena Huang unless the CEO responded by Wednesday, November 11, to a request for a closed-door briefing. No subpoena had been issued at the time of the report.

Mast and two other Republican committee chairs asked Huang to appear on November 18 and explain his interactions with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based chip buyer Megaspeed. The lawmakers also want details about Nvidia's safeguards against foreign espionage, according to a letter reviewed by Reuters.

U.S. authorities have reportedly investigated whether Alice Huang, who has no family relationship to the Nvidia CEO, passed information to Beijing. The allegations have not established that Jensen Huang knowingly disclosed confidential information or violated export rules.

On Sunday, Nvidia's board announced an independent investigation into whether its chief executive was targeted by a foreign intelligence operation. The board said it had no reason to believe that national security or company secrets were compromised, but would examine the available information.

Earlier today, the Financial Times reported Nvidia has hired former U.S. prosecutor Joon H Kim to conduct the internal investigation.

The company is also facing legal pressure. Shareholders filed a proposed class-action lawsuit Monday alleging that Nvidia and Huang failed to disclose risks associated with the reported relationship.

The scandal has landed during a broader reassessment of the AI boom. Investors have questioned how long massive spending on data centers can continue, and Nvidia's competitors are offering customers more alternatives to its expensive chips.

Google introduced new AI chip configurations Monday. The Information reported that OpenAI was considering buying Google's tensor processing units, or TPUs, which are processors built specifically to run AI workloads. Neither company has confirmed a purchase but yesterday Google announced a new line of TPUs available for select customers, directly competing against Nvidia’s chips.

Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly earnings on November 17, one day before the congressional briefing requested by House Republicans. Mast's deadline for Huang to respond comes first, on November 11.

#4979. Everybody Wants to Do Shabbat Dinner at Erin Foster and Simon Tikhman’s Cozy, Playful LA Home

The recently dropped third season of the hit Netflix rom-com Nobody Wants This begins with a picture of domestic bliss: Kristen Bell and Adam Brody’s characters cheerfully lugging boxes and eating pizza sans dishes—living together at last. The fictional romance between the witty podcaster and “hot rabbi” is inspired by that of series creator Erin Foster and her husband Simon Tikhman, albeit loosely. Like Bell’s Joanne, Foster converted to Judaism before their wedding in 2019; unlike Brody’s Noah, Tikhman is a music executive, not a rabbi—but art is still intermingling with life.

As their onscreen alter egos feather their nest, Foster, Tikhman, and their two-year-old daughter Noa are starring in a new season of their own, moving into a 1920s Los Angeles home that mixes undefinable colors and bold pattern play with the “European flavor” of an old-world estate, per Tikhman. Researching the house’s roots, Foster found Spanish, Mediterranean revival, and English influences, and the couple felt free to embrace all three.

“I don’t tend to like following a set of rules,” Foster says, “whether it be in fashion or in television writing or in design.”

To create more of a grand entrance, the entry foyer was opened up on the second floor and “guests often mistake the entry tile as original,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The stair runner is by Stark and the vintage mirror is from Nickey Kehoe. The walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Soft Chamois.

Tikhman, Foster, and Noa seated on the custom living room sofas upholstered in a Schumacher fabric. “I had girls over for drinks and we sat in the room together,” Foster says. “It’s kind of like the gossip room.”

The sofas flank a custom coffee table in the living room. The vintage chair is from 1stDibs, the ottoman is upholstered in a fabric by Scalamandré, and the vintage floor lamp is from PRB Collection. The sconces are from Aqua Gallery and the curtain fabric is by Rose Tarlow Melrose House. The chandelier is by Cox London. “They handmake each metal leaf,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The artwork is by Danielle Orchard from Perrotin Gallery.

The multihyphenate cohost of The World’s First Podcast and cofounder of cool-girl fashion line Favorite Daughter acknowledges that she runs in a “circle of really fashion-forward women who have been featured in AD and have elevated taste in art and in design, and it’s very intimidating,” including when it came to selecting a designer. “There starts to be this culture of, ‘Well, you have to use this person,’” Foster says. Instead, she discovered her own on Instagram. Foster cold-messaged Joyce Pickens Dinwiddy, founder of LA’s JDP Interiors (and later the brains behind Favorite Daughter’s Beverly Hills boutique) about masterminding the new-old home’s four-year renovation and redesign.

“They’re a cool couple and they don’t want this stuffy, old-school, fully British-feeling home,” Pickens Dinwiddy says, but Foster and Tikhman still craved classicism their way—something “cozy, young, and playful but sophisticated at the same time.”

“I’ve realized I’m obsessed with the color green,” Foster says. “The color feels a little shocking sometimes to people, but it’s also inviting and it’s also kind of earthy and cozy.” The kitchen is painted in Denali Green from Dunn-Edwards and the cabinets are backed in a Rose Tarlow Melrose House fabric.

Tikhman requested and received a dedicated drinks fridge in the kitchen, filled with clean sodas and seltzers. “Simon was like, ‘I want a Kardashian fridge,’” Foster says.

The kitchen backsplash includes custom hand-painted tiles by Lascaux Tile and the hood is copper. The range is by Lacanche, the fridge is Sub-Zero, and the sink fittings are by Waterstone. The pendants are custom.

The result is a warm, hip house where Pickens Dinwiddy, who’s married to a Brit, introduced charming flourishes familiar to UK country homes. She softened cabinetry in the not-quite olive green kitchen with floral chintz fabric by Rose Tarlow Melrose House and added a skirt beneath the sink in the scullery, a hybrid mud room/potting room traditionally used to water herb pots from the garden. The glass-ceilinged breakfast nook—an “architectural feat,” according to Pickens Dinwiddy, that was lowered into the home via crane and which Foster says “probably added a year to the project”—has been crowned the solarium. With sun drenching its slate blue walls and sculpted medallion details, “it’s a really special place for us to have early dinner with our daughter,” Foster says, and also where she pops open her laptop, morning coffee in hand.

“Erin likes to wake up, have her coffee, pull up her computer, and sit in a room,” Tikhman says. “And her dream was that’s what the solarium was going to be.” The custom banquette is upholstered in a fabric from Perennials Fabrics and topped with pillows covered in Robert Kime fabric. The chairs by Casamidy are upholstered in a Perennials by Rose Tarlow fabric. The pendant is by Vaughan.

“I wanted to do wall-to-wall paneling somewhere,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. “This felt like the place for it so that there wasn’t a wall treatment competing with this beautiful tile floor.” The floor tile is by Ann Sacks. The mudroom bench is upholstered in a fabric by Décors Barbares.

The custom dining room table is surrounded by custom chairs covered in a Jasper fabric. The 1950s chandelier is from Fargo Design and the vintage Swedish sconces are from 1stDibs. The 1970s Danish sideboard is from Chairish and the mirror is custom.

I revel in the regal room names, but the couple is decidedly unfussy about their solarium and scullery—“words that Erin and I never used before building this house,” Tikhman says. “We actually only speak with English accents when we’re at home,” she cracks.

In fact, Foster applauds Tikhman for gently voicing concerns when, in the throes of the project, her old-world sensibilities started to feel a bit too old. Or as Tikhman jokes: “You wanted parking for our horse and carriage.”

“The man cave is my solarium,” Tikhman says. “It was my nonnegotiable, but with Erin’s blessing too.” The sofa is upholstered in Forenza Cinnamon velvet by Romo and the pillows are covered in a fabric by Zak + Fox. The coffee table is vintage.

The bar in the den features custom barstools covered in a Rogers & Goffigon fabric. The table lamp is vintage, the sink fittings are by Waterworks, and the walls are painted in Galway Bay by Dunn-Edwards.

“Simon was like, ‘Listen, I love you. I trust you. I know that you’re going to make this house beautiful, but I just keep thinking that I’m going to be walking around in basketball shorts and sneakers and a backwards hat and you’re in your pajamas all day long. Are we going to be in a castle that doesn’t feel like our personalities?’” Foster recalls. “It really woke me up to this idea that you have to balance everything.”

Out went an old-fashioned pull-chain toilet in the powder room; in came modern Swedish and Italian light fixtures, plus Tikhman’s passion project: making theirs a stealth smart home.

“The bedroom is very important because we’re people who like to be in the bedroom,” Tikhman says. He issues a clarification: “We like to watch TV in bed.” The bed is upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric and flanked with custom sconces. The shade fabric is by Jasper and the sofa is upholstered in a Claremont Furnishing fabric. The vintage pendant is from 1stDibs and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Gray Wisp.

In the primary bathroom, the hand-painted wall mural is by James Mobley. The vanity pendants are custom.

The vintage marble cabochon tiles are from Neolithic Materials. The tub is by The Water Monopoly.

Behind peony-printed Papier wallpaper and vintage sconces from 1stDibs, “the brains of the house are so advanced,” says Tikhman, cofounder and CEO of The Core Entertainment, a music company that includes artist management, publishing, a record label, and brand partnerships. He’s giddy over no-show speakers baked into the walls, a dining room mirror that slides open to reveal a TV viewable from the kitchen—so he can simultaneously watch and chat with Foster as she cooks her go-to tofu with broccoli (“Soy is no longer being vilified,” she says)—and an AI assistant named Nicola who can modulate temperature, lights, and of course music.

In the primary suite sitting room, the chair is upholstered in a Schumacher velvet and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Mustang.

Tikhman’s shoe collection in the primary closet

Alongside the revamp of their family home, the couple collaborated on season three of Nobody Wants This, with Foster writing, executive-producing, and making a cameo as an unhinged podcast fan (her impersonations in the writer’s room demanded to be seen), and Tikhman executive-producing the season-three soundtrack, curating unreleased tracks from the likes of Miley Cyrus and Ellie Goulding for the rom-com’s sweeping, big-love moments. Tikhman is used to managing artists, but jokes that producing the show’s soundtrack for the second time was tougher. “I want to make sure that my wife and her team are happy, and managing Erin is way more difficult,” he grins. “She’s my artist.”

“I’m trying to dress [Noa] into a cool tomboy, but she seems to really love putting on lots of jewelry and carrying a handbag,” Foster says. “I think we’ll adjust the room based on who she becomes.” In the nursery, the daybed is upholstered in a fabric by Namay Samay. The walls are covered in Lover’s Bloom by Papier and the pendant is by Beauvamp.

In Noa’s bathroom, the Hector Finch sconces feature hand-painted shades by artist Sarah Blomfield. The sink fittings are by Waterworks, the pendant is vintage, and the floor tiles are by Tabarka.

In a recent episode of Nobody Wants This, a pool party at Joanne and Noah’s spins out of control. The couple balks at first, but “I kinda like being the house that everybody comes to,” Joanne decides. Foster and Tikhman emphatically agree. “We do not want an untouchable home,” Foster says. “I don’t want to be a coaster house. I’m okay with a ring on the table.”

For a recent San Francisco 49ers game, Tikhman invited friends and Noa to “soft-launch” his “man cave,” a freestanding structure lined in Forenza Cinnamon velvet sofas by Roma meant to evoke the “Ralph Lauren vibes” of a high-end bar.

Foster says sharing a home with Tikhman “unlocked my inner trad-wife hostess.”

“I’m on a group chat called Shabbat Moms,” she says. Of its 12 members, “we’ve probably hosted more than anybody. We’ll have 30 people over, and there’s kids running everywhere, and it’s the best kind of chaos ever.”

Foster, Tikhman, and Noa in the garden designed by Emily Cooper with Balanced Ground Design.

#4978. Your Guide to the 2026 NWSL Playoffs: Teams, Schedule, and How to Watch

After a long season, the stage is set for the National Women’s Soccer League playoffs. Eight teams will compete to see who will be crowned the 2026 champions at the end of the month. And there's been plenty of impressive soccer leading up to this point.

This year, the Washington Spirit's Ashley Sanchez tied the single-season league record with 20 goals to get the Golden Boot award. (Croix Bethune of the Kansas City Current set a league record for the most assists, too.)

Angel City FC went into the postseason with an impressive 10-match unbeaten streak, making them a big team to watch. The Seattle Reign also locked down the 8th spot in the playoffs in dramatic fashion with a 1-0 win over the Orlando Pride.

There were also big changes made to future player contracts, with the NWSL dropping the so-called "Rodman rule" that allows any player in the league to earn the NWSL's highest possible salaries. That goes into effect on January 1.

Finally, the regular season wrapped up with Decision Day on November 1, in which all of the final games kick off at once (whoa). When all was said and done, Gotham FC won the NWSL Shield—more on that in a minute—and all remaining teams locked in their seeding for playoffs.

So, which teams are in contention and how is this all going to go down? Whether you've never seen a NWSL game or need to brush up on the facts, we've got you covered.

The NWSL hosts single-elimination playoffs, which means if a team loses once, they're out. You can compare this to the NFL, whereas leagues like the WNBA, NBA, NHL, and MLB play a best-of series in each round to give teams multiple chances to advance.

This is a little confusing, so bear with us. The NWSL Shield is a regular-season honor that culminates at the end of the year.

The Shield is given to the team that has the best record over the regular season, which began back in March. Now, the playoffs begin, and the winner of the playoff tournament wins the NWSL Championship. It's possible to win both the Shield and the Championship, btw. It's just that the Shield is a distinction for regular season play. (Gotham FC won the NWSL Championship in 2023 and 2025, but only won the club’s first-ever Shield this season.)

The team that wins the Shield (in this case, Gotham) is also granted the No. 1 seed in the playoffs, which means they get home-field advantage during the postseason.

If there's a tie in the NWSL playoffs, the game will go on to extra time. This is two 15-minute periods that are played in their entirety. Whoever has the highest score at the end wins.

If things are still tied at that point, this goes to a penalty shootout. This is a best of five round of kicks, followed by sudden-death rounds if they're needed.

The first round of the playoffs will be broadcast on ESPN platforms, along with Prime Video, CBS, and Paramount+. The semifinals, which take place November 14-15, will be broadcast on ESPN networks, CBS, and Paramount+. The championship game will air on CBS and Paramount+.

You might even consider visiting a dedicated women’s sports bar in your area to watch with other like-minded fans—here’s an exhaustive list.

Eight teams are in contention this year. Here they are, as well as their seeds going into the first round:

The championship game is on Saturday, Nov. 21 at 8 pm ET. Last year’s championship game saw Gotham FC beat the Washington Spirt 1-0 with a goal at the 80th minute. The stage is set for an equally exciting finale to the NWSL season.

#4977. Trump Media Posts $410 Million Profit. Bitcoin Gains Did the Heavy Lifting

Trump Media Posts $410 Million Profit. Bitcoin Gains Did the Heavy Lifting

Trump Media & Technology Group reported a $409.7 million third-quarter profit on Monday, a sharp turnaround for the company behind President Donald Trump's Truth Social.

However, most of the money that Trump Media & Technology made didn't come from media or technology. It came from the rising value of its crypto holdings and related investments.

The company generated just $2.97 million in revenue during the three months ended September 30, according to its SEC filing. A year earlier, it had reported a $54.8 million loss.

Trump Media recorded $496.1 million in realized and unrealized gains on digital assets, pledged crypto and equity securities, according to its earnings release. Much of that was an accounting gain—assets rose in value even when the company hadn't sold them for cash.

Digital assets and pledged coins accounted for $378.1 million of those gains, with securities adding another $118 million. Derivative losses and other expenses offset part of the windfall.

Bitcoin supplied a major boost. Chief Financial Officer Phillip Juhan said on the earnings call that it rose about 42% during the quarter, from roughly $59,000 at the end of June to $84,000 at the end of September.

Trump Media held approximately 15,649 Bitcoin at quarter-end, including around 11,385 coins pledged to a program designed to generate additional returns. Some of those assets are subject to arrangements allowing the counterparty to reuse the coins, according to the filing.

The company expanded into crypto in July 2025, purchasing about $2 billion in Bitcoin and related securities. That decision has tied its earnings closely to changes in digital-asset prices.

The downside was obvious just three months ago. Back in August Trump Media lost $238 million in the second quarter as declining crypto prices battered its holdings. This quarter, the accounting effect went in the opposite direction.

Truth Social's underlying business improved, though from a small base. Revenue increased 205% from about $973,000 a year earlier, supported by subscriptions for Truth+ streaming and Truth API, a paid service that gives customers faster access to posts on the platform that critics have likened to insider trading.

The company also recorded growth in advertising sales and fees from its Truth.Fi funds. Even so, Trump Media reported a $12.3 million adjusted EBITDA loss, a measure that excludes investment gains and certain other items, compared with a $29.1 million loss a year earlier.

The latest quarterly profit has not repaired the longer-term picture for shareholders. DJT shares traded around $4.20 Tuesday, up about 3.3% on the day but down 94.7% from their $79.38 registered back in 2024. The stock has lost nearly 70% this year.

Trump Media still posted a $234.2 million net loss for the first nine months of 2026. It is also pursuing a $6 billion merger with fusion-energy developer TAE Technologies, which shareholders are scheduled to vote on December 14.

Before that vote, holders of the company's $1 billion in convertible notes can demand cash repayment on November 30, according to its quarterly filing.

#4976. Hot US inflation raises odds of half-point Fed hike in December

US inflation came in above forecasts in October, increasing expectations of a bigger rate hike in December and raising concerns for crypto investors.

Crypto industry experts warned on Tuesday that higher-than-expected US inflation could lead to a another Federal Reserve interest rate hike, making borrowing more expensive and potentially discouraging investment in cryptocurrencies.

The US Consumer Price Index (CPI) rose 0.4% month over month in October, above economists’ forecast of 0.3%, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics - a slower increase than September’s 0.6% rise. Core inflation, which excludes food and energy prices, rose 0.3%, above the expected 0.2%.

Currently, the Federal Reserve holds its benchmark interest rate at 3.75% to 4.00%, after raising it by a quarter-point in September and leaving it unchanged in October.

The report came just a few hours after Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller said in London that another core inflation reading of 0.3% or higher could prompt the Fed to consider a larger interest rate hike in December.

“In that case, I believe a 50-basis-point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting,” Waller said. “Hiking only 25-basis-points would put our credibility at risk, a risk I am not eager to take.”

Markets were already pricing a quarter-point Fed hike in December. Tuesday’s data and Waller’s remarks are now raising the odds that the Fed would tighten monetary policy more aggressively in December, potentially raising borrowing costs and squeezing returns for leveraged crypto investors.

Related: US midterm elections 2026 as it happened: Democrats win control of Congress

After the CPI release, traders were pricing in slightly more than a quarter point of Fed tightening for December, according to Jeff Anderson, head of US at crypto trading firm STS Digital.

“Following today’s CPI release, markets are now pricing approximately 27 basis points of tightening into the December rate curve,” Anderson said in comments shared with Cointelegraph. “The prospect of another rate hike is becoming increasingly difficult to ignore.”

CME FedWatch data as of 16:28 UTC on Tuesday showed an 88.3% chance of a quarter-point rate hike in December and an 11.7% chance of a half-point increase. Meanwhile, the broader crypto market was trading lower following the report, with total market capitalization falling 1.6% over the past 24 hours to $2.57 trillion, according to CoinGecko.

Bitcoin (BTC) traded near $73,274, down 2% over the past 24 hours, while Ether (ETH) fell nearly 5% to around $2,028 and Solana (SOL) dropped 5.6% to $82.

Experts said that higher interest rates could also reduce profits from some crypto investments. Adam Haeems, head of asset management at Tesseract Group, told Cointelegraph that investors who borrow money to make other investments could earn less if borrowing becomes more expensive.

“Carry strategies earn the gap between what their assets yield and what they pay to borrow, so they are squeezed when borrowing costs rise relative to that yield,” Haeems said. “Over the past two weeks the rate paid to USDC lenders on Aave’s main market ranged from about 3.2% to 7%, while three-month Treasury yields stayed close to 4.2%.”

The difference, in turn, could influence where investors put their money, especially if traditional investments offer better returns with less risk.

The inflation report also came a week after the US midterm elections, in which Democrats won control of both the House and Senate.

“There is also likely to be growing political pressure from a blue Congress to bring inflation under control, potentially reinforcing the case for higher rates,” Anderson said.

Still, he added that he remained skeptical about whether further rate hikes would address the underlying causes of inflation.

While Congress does not set interest rates, lawmakers can influence economic policy and crypto regulation. For instance, the CLARITY Act, a bill aimed at creating clearer crypto rules for the US, has been stalled since January amid disagreements between Democrats and Republicans.

The shift in congressional power combined with the possibility of higher borrowing costs could create even more uncertainty for crypto investors, experts said.
